ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u1	The next item is the debate on the draft law 35154, the adoption of the Comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement (CETA) between Canada, of the one part, and the European Union and its Member States, of the other part, and the draft law 35155, the adoption of the Strategic Partnership Agreement between the European Union and its Member States, of the one part, and Canada, of the other part, established in Brussels on 30 October 2016.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u2	I give the floor to Mrs Karimi.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u3	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Greta Thunberg was 14 years old when she sat in front of the Swedish Parliament and sat there for days alone with a sign on which she herself wrote: school strike for the climate. Greta, a teenage girl, had no money, no army of lobbyists or spokesmen or any other power instrument. A young girl, worried about her own future, about the future of her peers and about our planet, decided not to let things go. The beauty of her extremely simple and brilliant action inspired millions of people, especially young people, and made her a leader of the climate movement. With her school strike, she literally and figuratively stepped out of the inevitable-like treadmill of our time. Global warming and the consequences of climate change cannot be ignored. With an average age of 61 in this Chamber, we may not be experiencing the most disastrous consequences of climate change, but our children and grandchildren will. But even now extreme floods, drought and forest fires destroy lives of millions of people. These extreme weather conditions occur not only in Africa or Asia, but also increasingly in Europe. Biodiversity is shrinking at a very rapid rate. That's the other silent disaster that threatens Earth's vitality. What does climate change and declining biodiversity have to do with a trade and investment agreement between the EU and Canada? Everything. The dominant economic model that since the beginning of industrialization has put man, animal and nature at the service of profit maximization and certainly the neoliberalism of recent decades have caused this crisis. But poverty and extreme inequality in the world are also consequences of the neoliberal economy. Mr. Chairman. We can pretend that there is no climate crisis that threatens humanity's existence. We can close our eyes and ears to the crying of our children and grandchildren, who live with existential uncertainty about their future. We can pretend that there is no growing inequality between rich and poor and we can pretend that the minuscule improvements in the neoliberal economy of recent decades are sufficient to reverse the tide. I still hear Greta Thunberg's raging cry against the world leaders, gathered in the United Nations in 2019: how dare you. The Broad Economic and Trade Agreement (CETA) concluded between the EU and its Member States on the one hand and Canada on the other is the most comprehensive and ambitious trade agreement in the history of the EU. The Convention removes trade barriers such as non-tariff barriers and customs duties at 98% of products and on all industrial goods. It also provides for the establishment of rules of origin, strengthens access to services and opens up the procurement market. In addition to trade, CETA regulates investment protection. The negotiations and the signing on 30 October 2016 took place at the height of neoliberalism, before a pandemic exposed the vulnerabilities and dependencies of international production chains and globalisation, before Russia's aggressive war against Ukraine turned world food supply into a weapon of war and before the alarming IPCC reports and extreme weather conditions put the urgency of climate change on the agenda. Mr. Chairman. In CETA, two central principles of neoliberalism have been worked out. One: more growth and globalisation are good. Two: the State has the task of maximum facilitation of the market and businesses. This means maximum liberalisation of trade in goods and services and protection of businesses up to and including the creation of a special court system to protect investment by companies and multinationals. At the same time, these are my group's central criticisms of this treaty. In preparing this debate, we have asked hundreds of questions in this Chamber. This makes the content and scope of the Convention clear. I am not going to ask a lot of questions about that today. Today, as far as my group is concerned, it is our political judgment. Let me be clear: this debate is not about Canada. Canada is a democratic rule of law, similar to ours. Human rights and progressive values are often at the heart of this. There is no doubt about it: Canada is and remains a valued partner with or without CETA. That is why my group will vote for the adoption of the Strategic Partnership Agreement between the European Union and Canada, which is also on the agenda today. Mr. Chairman. The Paris climate agreement made ambitious commitments to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. GroenLinks expects that all public trading is at the service of combating climate change and ensuring that all layers of society can participate in the transition in a socially just way. The European Commission's impact analysis states literally that the increase in trade will lead to increased greenhouse gas emissions. An example of what the removal of restrictions under this Convention could mean is that tar sand oil and shale gas can be exported to the European Union. Both fossil energy sources are extremely bad for the climate and their production has a devastating effect on the environment, nature and the living environment of humans and animals. LTO Nederland argues passionately for CETA. Their letter of lobbying further confirmed my group's conviction against this treaty. I quote from the letter: "Since the Convention entered into force in 2017, exports to Canada have increased more rapidly than imports. Also for pig farming, for example." More and more pork and beef go from the Netherlands to Canada, while the Dutch nitrogen policy requires a halving of livestock. In the broadcast of Nieuwsuur on 7 July last, it was made clear that it is not farmers who benefit from more production and more exports. The agri-industrial sector around the farmers is the great profiteer. They make a lot of money and hunt the production. With this, they are partly responsible for cutting down even more square kilometres of rainforest in the Amazon. Soy from Brazil is processed into feed to send the meat produced in the Netherlands back across the ocean to Canada. And that at a time when protests by farmers against nitrogen reduction paralyse part of the country. This is the long international production chain we're talking about. It is a model example of the perverse consequences of neoliberal globalisation with trade agreements such as CETA. A way out of the current agricultural crisis in the Netherlands is to set quality above quantity and ensure that the farmer gets a fair price. 2,500 farmers presented a ten-point plan last week in response to the current agricultural crisis. CETA, which exposes these farmers to competition from Canada through the import of cheap products, where standards are also a little lower, does not help the transition to sustainable agriculture. CETA keeps farmers trapped in a system that damages the environment, soil and water quality, and has to produce animals cheaply for the world market. Above all, we see the negative effects of neoliberal globalisation. The local economy needs to increasingly compete with the global economy, making cheap production the main goal. As a result, social rights and labour rights are under pressure. We all know the erosion of labour rights and the exploitation of people in modern slavery in many sectors, such as the textile industry. Mr. Chairman. Our second major objection concerns the great power of large international companies and multinationals as a result of this treaty. The investment arbitrage ICS of CETA provides foreign investors with rights that domestic entrepreneurs do not have and allows foreign investors to cross national courts. The choice lies entirely with the companies. In the event of a dispute, they may decide to follow the path of national justice or that of the ICS. Although the ICS is less bad than the old Investor-State Dispute Settlement, the so-called ISDS, in trade agreements, because it has more transparency and is permanently working with fixed judges, we, GroenLinks, even after several written rounds, remain concerned with why the EU and Canada, with strong constitutional traditions and independent justice, need separate courts outside national or European judicial systems to settle disputes between companies and governments.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u4	I leave a lot of points that you put under neoliberalism to rest. I think you have called it six times. I am now talking about the issue we are discussing in this Chamber, namely dispute resolution. I believe you say, unless I have misunderstood you, that the ICS forum would not be accessible to small businesses. That seems to me to be factually wrong. It's open to everyone. It is only a choice whether you want to use the local rights or the arbitration forum. Did I interpret that correctly or did I not understand correctly? What is it?

I told them that these companies can choose whether to go to a national or an international court. But also on the basis of the experience we have with existing treaties, I believe that large companies and multinationals will make use of ICS in particular. Or does Mr. Backer fight that?
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u6	I don't fight anything, but ICS hasn't been tested in practice yet. The fact that multinational companies have used ICSID in the past, which is quite scarce, will have something to do with the fact that they work in several countries, often make larger investments and thus have an interest in conducting such arbitration proceedings which are unlikely to be conducted in local courts or surrounded by very few safeguards. So again, it's not just for big companies. I can imagine larger companies using it earlier, but why would it disqualify the whole?
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u7	That's not the point of our objection at all. It doesn't matter if they're big or small companies. We simply have independent justice, a European judicial system and a Canadian judicial system, which we can simply rely on. Those companies can go there. They do not need to have a separate international route. The point made by Mr Backer about investing in several countries is correct, but disputes are always about a very specific country. I will give the example of the Netherlands in a moment with the Coal Act. You know that the energy charter has been used and that processes have been carried out against the Netherlands, against a national state. That might as well have happened in the Netherlands.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u8	Finally, Mr. Backer.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u9	Finally, Mr President. Now I know GroenLinks as very constitutional. We also had debates together on Poland, Hungary and the rule of law. Canada enters into a treaty with 26 countries in Europe. Of those 26 countries, I think there are very few where she or I, or perhaps you, would dare to sue. Then, after all, investment protection would be useful if I did not let the countries mention it all, to avoid some courts and to ensure that you have fair investment protection? These investments must, of course, benefit the citizens of that country. That's what it's for.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u10	Mr Backer really disappoints me now. Yes, indeed, we are working together against violations of the rule of law in Poland and Hungary within the EU. A solution must also be found, but that cannot be an excuse to come up with a separate route to protect the investments of large companies! So where do we end up? Are we going to set up separate tribunals and courts everywhere? No, we have to address the problem of Poland and Hungary wherever it belongs, and we do.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u11	You will continue your speech, Mrs Karimi.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u12	Yes. Where was I? Oh, yeah. In fact, we believe that the creation of the ICS contributes to the crumbling of our democratic system because the ICS serves too much private business and too little public interest. This is also evident from past experience with ISDS disputes. In view of his experience with ISDS and energy charter, the Netherlands should go fiercely against ICS. The Netherlands, on the basis of the energy charter, has billions of claims to pay due to the recent coal law, with two German energy giants, RWE and Uniper, demanding 1.4 billion and 1 billion euros respectively because the Netherlands prohibits the generation of electricity from coal burning by 2030. This is inconvenient for governments and countries that want to pursue a more socially equitable and sustainable policy to achieve the Paris climate goals. Is the Netherlands willing to commit itself to reforming the Energy Charter? Investment in energy must not stand in the way of climate protection. Mr. Chairman. I'm going back to CETA. Then there is the transfer of legislative powers to the CETA Committees, to the semi-soft environmental provisions and social provisions in them. The European Parliament and the national parliaments are out of play and there is too much power in CETA committees to implement, for example, investment protection. The Committees are not sufficiently legally secure and transparent to pursue the general interest. The risk of lowering standards through the committees is also lurking. This gives international companies and multinationals a lot of extra power. They are informed about legislative projects early in this so-called regulatory cooperation. We know how strong the lobbying power of the companies in Brussels and The Hague is. It is precisely this minister who knows a great deal about this as a former EU Member of Parliament. Businesses can influence new proposals early in the legislative process unnoticed. The intended harmonisation of laws and rules can be harmonised to a lower level without the influence of parliaments, the European Parliament or the national parliaments. The case of Neelie Kroes, employed by Uber, published by independent journalists yesterday, shows that companies are doing everything they can to influence rules and laws to their advantage. We must not give multinationals more power. In this context, I would also like to briefly mention the complaints mechanism for complaints by civil society organisations, for which the Netherlands has argued. The fact is, as the government wrote in response to our questions, that with this hotline no iota changes to CETA. It is as the environmental organisations and FNV wrote to us in their letter: "Sustainability Complaints Report is merely a patchwork against ICS and weak sustainability chapters." Let me repeat the words of Jan Terlouw, honorary member of D66, and Jan Pronk, former Minister of the PvdA, in the article of 10 July. "It is therefore wise for the First Chamber to vote against CETA. This will allow for new global commitments to address the major challenges of today, such as the climate crisis, declining biodiversity, food security, sustainability of economic transactions and poverty and inequality. When concluding trade agreements, let the EU look at the impact on the rest of the world." Mr. Chairman. I'm closing up.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u14	No. No, no, no, no! I'm closing up, Mr. Chairman.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u16	My youngest grandson, Sami, is exactly two months old today. Looking at him and his three years older brother Kian, I increasingly wonder which world she and all their peers will live in. What world are we leaving behind? Society-destructive effects of climate change and inequality are already evident. Business as usual is no longer an option. The continuation of neoliberal economics is no longer possible. More, more and more growth. More, more and more exhaustion of the earth. More, more and more production and consumption. More, more and more exports. It has to stop. This treaty is good for everything that stands for the economy of "more, more and more" and bad, very bad, for the much needed transition to a fairer, more social and more sustainable world. For Sami, Kian and all the other children and young people for whom Greta Thunberg literally stepped out of the system, my group will vote with conviction against the CETA Convention. ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u92	Mr President, thank you. Just a heads-up. Of course, Mrs Karimi and I could have chosen to speak together today, but that might have been a bridge too far. I see Mr Koole looking my way. Mr. Chairman. For years, from left to right, we know that hyperglobalisation has no future anymore. This was already the case for the disruptive effects of the current geopolitical crisis, as well as for the corona pandemic. That this form of globalisation brings us more than good, we know for sure since the previous global crisis, the financial crisis of 2008. That crisis was the direct result of neoliberal hyperglobalisation. Even the hyperglobalists of the past have come to realize in the years after the financial crisis that the free market and globalisation as a project of the further release of that market on a global scale are not blissful. Yes, neoliberal politicians, who have always embraced this globalisation, have come to realize that it is also accompanied by growing inequality, social disruption and harmful effects for people and the environment, effects that are not included as costs in the capitalist free market model. We all know this a long time ago. The corona crisis has once again magnified the way in which this form of globalisation is at the expense of our planet and our broad prosperity. We also know that if we opt for a globalisation in which multinationals are placed above people, people will no longer accept it at some point. This is a form of globalisation that undermines and disrupts democracy. And yet, unfortunately, we have to take serious account of the fact that a majority of this Chamber is considering voting tomorrow, after a long debate today, before this obsolete treaty from the era of neoliberal hyperglobalisation, the so-called free trade agreement with Canada. With this, the PvdA Senate Group seems to finally say goodbye to the slogan and the ideal that we have previously joined in the joint fight against the previous Rutte cabinet, namely: people over multinationals. I deeply regret that. Because this CETA Convention is doing exactly the opposite. It places multinationals above people. This threatens tomorrow, in our Senate, to become a black day for all those who fight for fair world trade and for a fairer world. An investor who goes to the ICS excludes any review of national laws or treaties, such as human rights treaties or the Paris Climate Convention. Another serious objection from my group to CETA is that it has also been mentioned earlier about this, and yet in another way it undermines democracy. CETA is a so-called "living treaty" aimed at increasingly harmonising regulation on the Canadian and European sides. This could mean Canada adopting European standards, but also the other way around. The problem here is that we, the Dutch legislature, as laid down in the Constitution, have nothing to say about this. This is the case, although it may well involve a form of regulatory adjustment. The Convention delegates independent powers to the so-called Joint Committee and to shady working groups, consisting of EU and Canadian officials. We cannot know who these officials are. The unelected Joint Committee is not merely a consultative body, but, as several legal scholars have argued, actually exercises legislative powers. In answering our questions, the Minister acknowledges that neither national parliaments nor the European Parliament have any involvement in this. Nor can they exercise any control over these supranational committees. Can the Minister explain again why she does not see this as an undermining of our democracy? Mr. Chairman. The fact that CETA is a neo-liberal treaty is also reflected in the way everything is aimed at liberalisation. Only public services that are excluded in advance and on a so-called negative list will not be liberalised. For everything that's left, it's automatically open to Canadian investors. Once liberalisation has started, it cannot be reversed. For example, if the Netherlands had a law stating that in a particular sector no more than 30% foreign property is allowed, and this would then be increased by that country to 40%, then this could not be reversed later. That's weird, isn't it? 'Cause maybe there could be really good reasons for wanting to take it back. But even if it is our own democratic decision, that is not possible. Once liberalised, it has always been liberalised. Do you see why business and, in particular, large companies have lobbied so hard for CETA and are now so strongly advocating ratification? If you are wondering where this example comes from: it comes from the government itself; it is in the response. CETA ensures that what is already deregulated can no longer be reregulated. That is the so-called standstill clause. If a country decides at some point to liberalise further, this cannot be reversed either. That's the so-called ratchet mechanism. The Government's further reply to the Court explains this neatly. I quote: 'The standstill mechanism and the ratchet mechanism thus define, on the one hand, the existing degree of liberalisation at the time of entry into force of the Treaty and, on the other hand, ensure that future unilateral liberalisations are automatically part of the Treaty'. So that's what I call a neoliberal treaty. But above all, it is also an undemocratic treaty, a treaty that places our democracy partly on sale for the interests of multinationals, because it restricts our future policy autonomy. Does the Minister agree with me that this will restrict our policy autonomy? And yes, the public services on negative lists are excluded, but this is not just about the question: what do you keep completely public and what do you leave to the market? It is also about regulating the market, for example in the case of a pandemic or an energy crisis. I fully agree with Mr Koole that this is important. Mr. Chairman. The last part of my speech contains for those who place people and our democracy above multinationals, more than enough reason to put CETA here in the Senate tomorrow. But the SP Group has more objections. I'll be a little shorter on that, given the time, but also because others here, I think, will still think about it. CETA is not only a neo-liberal but also a fossil treaty. As a result of CETA, CO emissions are not only increasing in any way, which is also reflected in the EU's own studies. The recently discussed investment protection in CETA also protects investments by the fossil industry, including Canada's large oil industry, including its most polluting form: tar sand oil. That is not from this time, I ask the Minister. This is entirely in line with Mrs Karimi's speech. How does this relate to the climate objectives of this cabinet? Now let's really go dough over our planet? Mr. Chairman. As far as the commercial part of CETA is concerned, the government and other proponents have often thrown sand into our eyes. At least an attempt has been made to do so by constantly emphasising that we do not have to worry about the liberalised imports of Canadian products, because they simply have to keep up with European standards. Apart from the fact that we do not know whether these standards could be reduced in the future in the framework of CETA and by all those undemocratic committees and working groups, and even the European precautionary principle could be called into question, it is not just product standards. At least that should not be the only issue in trade. Fair trade should not be limited to the question of which quality requirements, for example which food safety requirements, the goods to be imported should be met. Fair trade should also enable countries to make demands on production methods. How is what is on our plate produced, for example? Contrary to what is often said, Canada is not at all such a progressive country in this area. Canada is also well behind in some environmental and animal welfare requirements. Does the Minister recognize that? Does the Minister recognise that, in principle, it would be good if we looked not only at product requirements in world trade, but also at production requirements, so that differences in the way we produce can be reflected in higher rates? All this has not been taken into account in CETA, because almost all tariffs are completely abolished, including in the agricultural sector. My group believes that this is a bad thing as long as the standards in Canada, for example in the field of animal welfare, are far from being at our level. The fact that Canadian farmers do not have to meet the same requirements as our farmers, I understand that they have now also arrived in the Kazernestraat, which means, of course, unfair competition. It means that farmers who, quite rightly, have to meet ever higher environmental and animal welfare requirements within CETA do not have a level playing field. Therefore, it is not for nothing that several farmers' organisations are fiercely opposed to CETA. Mr. Chairman. I should like to conclude my speech. The key question is: do we want to go forward or backwards? Do we opt for an essentially reactionary free trade and investment treaty from the era of long-disrupted hyperglobalisation? Or do we opt for progress, for fair world trade, for trade agreements with respect for our planet, for humans and for animals? Mr. Chairman. Proponents call CETA a must, because Canada would be such a progressive country, a country with which we share so many values. That may be so, but CETA's values are far from progressive. If those values are good to us, let's stop with CETA. If we think people are going over multinationals, let's stop with CETA. In fact, I still hope that the PvdA Group will agree. We have just heard that, but that is not clear from their explanation of vote. The minister will be very excited, but many PvdA voters who thought the PvdA would choose the right thing to do here have checked. The vote isn't until tomorrow. Today we heard the protest outside. Many PvdA members are also stirring. With them, I call once again on the PvdA Group to vote against this neoliberal and fossil treaty. In the meantime, I look forward to the Minister's reply.

Under investment protection, these are civil claims, which can also be called claims. Article 112, first paragraph, of the Constitution provides that the jurisdiction of disputes concerning civil rights and claims shall be conferred on the judiciary. By accepting a parallel legal system, the ICS, parts of civil law, which fall within the jurisdiction of the Dutch courts, are transferred to international judges. This is also a derogation from the Constitution. In the event that a treaty contains provisions derogating from the Constitution, a two-thirds majority in both chambers is required for ratification. It is therefore not easy to understand that this treaty came through the House of Representatives by a narrow majority. So CETA will not provide us with a stream of care staff from Canada. Whoever works in home care knows that lonely elderly people would rather have a human being than a robot around them. How does the cabinet prevent these jobs from competing with those in health care and education? Then there is the third CETA target: lower consumer prices for some products. This can be desirable if society wants to encourage people to consume more of these products, for example from the point of view of health prevention or sustainability. I have found nowhere that imports from Canada are about such products. On the contrary. Suppose you do find the goals of CETA worthy. Is CETA a suitable means of achieving this? The Consumers' Union, the FNV, farmers' unions and environmental organisations do not think so and call on us to vote against ratification. VNO-NCW is in favour and sought publicity last week with a column entitled "Who dives well in CETA sees that old arguments do not apply." It is also said by other parties, at least by one opposition party in this Chamber. According to the employers' club, binding sustainability chapters would make CETA a climate mileage, but these chapters contain definitions such as: the Parties aim to promote sustainable development, promote the integration of sustainability considerations and improve compliance with international labour and environmental agreements. To improve alone! Not perfecting, but better than it is now. A sustainability chapter with such vague definitions and without concrete standards is nothing. The employers' association also reports that Canada has improved the rules for long-distance animal transport. That's right, but it's a little misleading. With this improvement, Canada is not even close to what we find acceptable in the Netherlands and the rest of the EU. In Canada, the maximum permitted journey time without food, drink and rest for cows, sheep and goats has fallen from 48 hours to 36 hours; even worse than in the US, where it is 28 hours. In the EU, the maximum distance is 8 hours. VNO-NCW is also wrong when it writes about one of the most criticized parts of CETA, investor protection, that it has been revolutionaryly renewed. Sure, there are differences between ICS and ISDS. In ICS, investors can only claim financial compensation, not withdrawal of measures. But that also reduces democratic freedom because governments consider the risk of compensation damage in policy. It could be paralyzed. The compensation to be paid also hinders democracy, because the government cannot spend taxpayers' money that has to be paid to investors on anything else, something that we as First Chamber with the Second Chamber democratically decide on. Investors have rights but no duties and cannot be charged. Arbiters also have a financial incentive in the ICS to judge in favour of investors. There is no requirement to be aware of issues such as human rights and financial stability. I'll come back to that last one. The compensation is based on lost future profits and can therefore lead to much higher amounts than possible through the national courts. My group colleague Peter Nicolaï has already referred to this. UNCTAD warns governments about megaclaims about corona damage. That's through ISDS. Lawyer offices are getting hot. That kind of claim can happen under ICS, can't it? I mean, if there is another pandemic in the future, for example. I also ask her whether she takes into account the possibility that investor protection will cost the Netherlands a lot of money. I asked Mr Backer in an interruption, because that was the case at the time, but it was also in my speech: is it true that if another EU country violates company rights, a Canadian investor does and a Dutch investor is not allowed to go to the ICS? I'd like to hear a confirmation from the Minister. Mr. Chairman. Dispute resolution through the International Court System. Point one. The many e-mails that we have received in recent years, of which 95% have been produced through copy-pastes, emphasise that trade agreements are made through back-room politics. This has been the subject of extensive attention in questions to the government and experts. The conclusion can only be that these are merely fables. The mandates are parliamentary and determine the discretion of a minister. The European Council framework then instructs the Commission on what is possible and what is not possible. The College of Commissioners is also a political body responsible to the Council of Ministers and the European Parliament. This is carefully documented. Of course, the negotiations themselves are held in the comfortless chambers of the Berlaymont or the Justus Lipsius building. Of course, officials are involved, but that does not make it undemocratic. If I had operated without any trouble or back-spoke at the time, Frits Bolkestein and Gerrit Zalm had rightly shown me all the corners of the room and I wouldn't be standing here today. That's the way it is with us, Mrs. Karimi. This trade agreement would once again involve the transfer of sovereignty. That sovereignty was already transferred at the time of the Treaty of Rome in 1957. This was necessary in order to achieve a customs union and then a single market. Is that bad? Absolutely not. For if the EEC is set up for something, it is to promote economic growth and employment through the conclusion of trade agreements and the creation of a common market of about 500 million consumers. If you want to regain powers, you will have to change the EU Treaty. The VVD will never participate in such a treaty amendment. It is, of course, an illusion to think that a president in China or in the United States, whatever president he is, would be more willing to compromise for access to a consumer market of 17 million consumers than for access to a market of 500 million consumers. The British, with a market of 60 million consumers, have suffered the negative effects since they left the EU. This is not only happening in their negotiations with the US, which London still thinks is a special relationship, quod non. Finally, Mr President. On this specific point, the Treaty would be contrary to the Constitution or would derogate from it. The Court of Justice in Luxembourg has ruled that the Treaty is fully in line with the EU Treaty and the Council of State, in an opinion of 28 November 2018, found that the provisions are in conformity with the Dutch Constitution, because the investor, or other interested parties, have the choice to refer their dispute to the national court or to the international Court System. Then there is the second issue. The second part of my speech is the economic importance. That would be negligiblely small. Only multinationals that do not already pay taxes are favoured. This is in no way proportionate to the great good of our own sovereignty. I've just spoken about the latter. I have always had difficulty following this reasoning. If the economic importance is negligible, then no one is favoured, not even multinationals. If there is no economic interest, neither can there be an increase in imports and exports, i.e. the undermining of quality standards and animal welfare and the promotion of improper competition. In short, if ratification does not have an economic impact, then there can be no disaster and misery that some people, even so-called experts, think they should describe. It's two-and-a-half. Mr. Chairman. Fortunately, in recent years, it has been shown that the treaty, which has been in force for five years, has indeed increased trade between Canada and the EU and that it does not mean that the Dutch economy will lose sight of it. The EU, and certainly the Netherlands, must have research, development and innovation. That is the only way we can stand up internationally. We cannot and do not want to influence our competitiveness by reducing environmental requirements and labour protection and laxity in the field of food safety and quality. The control of this is stepped up and is also partly in Dutch hands. Only those companies whose products meet European standards will be granted an export licence. In short, there is no chlorine chicken on the Dutch market because of this treaty. Nor is the Treaty in breach of European law. This has not only been clearly established at an expert meeting, but has also been confirmed in the opinion of the Council of State and in a ruling of the Court of Justice in Luxembourg following a Walloon politically motivated hiccup. It is also clear that the ICS system is a clear improvement of the Investor-State Dispute Settlement. The latter was highly critical of the lack of independence, scope and procedures. ICS is a great improvement, for which Mrs Ploumen made herself strong with the support of the Chamber at the time. ICS is a permanent court, independent and public. Moreover, the scope is limited in the sense, as stated above, that a government cannot be called upon to adopt policies that serve the general interest. The State can only be sued in the case of direct or indirect expropriation, in the case of one specific company, where reasons of general interest do not apply. My comment would be that in such circumstances it is a good thing that the State can be sued. However, it will be of minor importance, because we are not dealing with Canada as a criminal state. The expectation that a diarrhea of claims will come from the Netherlands is therefore not based on anything. CETA protects investors in jurisdictions that are less robust than the Canadian and the Dutch, to express it diplomatically. In other words, such investment protection can indeed be significant in some EU Member States. Experience also shows this, because so far only two claims have been made against the Dutch State. Two is rather modest in the context of 95 bilateral investment treaties that have been agreed. Anyway, Mr. President, let it be done.

Something has already been said this morning or the so-called'regulatory chill' effect, but I would like to make another point. How does Mr Van Ballekom view the so-called standstill clause and the ratchet clause in CETA? This is about the fact that, at the time CETA enters into force, the liberalisation that is already in force at that time actually applies forever. In fact, Mr Van Ballekom says that we can always change the treaty, but even if we cancel CETA, that investment protection still applies for a period of 20 years. So the standstill clause says: what is once liberalised remains liberalised. And the ratchet clause says: if you then continue to liberalise, that is not to be reversed. So if we as the Dutch Parliament were to decide that we really want to reverse it because we do not think that liberalisation is a good idea after all, it is not possible under the CETA Convention. I think that's a limitation of the right to regulate.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u308	We do not even need to look outside the EU's borders to see that it can be otherwise. NGOs and other civil society organisations can join such a process. There's a lot more to name. Mr. Chairman. In this context, I would like to ask the following question. Have I understood correctly from the documents that the ICS can grant a claim but has no influence on the rules and laws that a national government enacts, unlike the national court, which, in exceptional cases, may decide to terminate a rule? A widely heard objection is the so-called chilling effect: governments or parliaments that, for fear of billions of claims, already take too much account of the interests of multinationals with deep pockets when drafting legislation. We talked about that earlier this morning. That would indeed be a bad thing. But scientific research at the London School of Economics and Political Science leads to the conclusion that such an effect does not occur or to a very limited extent in practice. As a rule, governments are happy to choose the public interest, despite a threat of claims. In addition, in earlier investigations on arbitration cases, the government concerned was found to be equal in 60% of the cases. In other cases, a friendly settlement was generally reached. So we really do not have so much to fear. The right to regulate is laid down in the Treaty. It leaves the government the room to adopt laws that, while damaging investors, serve the public interest. In such a case, there can be no claim granted. The examples given in the interpretative document to that right are not exhaustive. This means, for example, that climate policy laws, although not mentioned in the list, are also included. That's what the Council of State says in its opinion. In this context, my group wonders where the final consideration lies whether a law serves the public interest. I would ask the Minister to comment on that. In a realistic assessment of this treaty, my group also wants to recognise that the Dutch economy, and therefore all the Dutch, not only entrepreneurs, have an interest in removing trade barriers. In addition, a treaty with Canada is a treaty with a similar country in terms of European values. This is very important in the changing geopolitical world. The European Union needs equal partners. Mr. Chairman. My group confines itself to the rules of this Convention when assessing CETA. However, we have noted with great satisfaction the efforts of the Dutch Government and the European Commission, among others, to put in place a complaint mechanism, the Single Entry Point. Although this mechanism is not part of CETA, it changes the position of NGOs and civil society organisations to the benefit of all trade agreements. This notification mechanism will give them more opportunities to address, for example, sustainability and human rights abuses in relation to the implementation of conventions. Although the SEP mechanism does not specifically focus on CETA, it will also strengthen the position of these groups under this Convention. My group also agrees with what Mr Koole has said on this subject. Mr. Chairman. This was my contribution and I look forward to the Minister's answers.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u309	Thank you. I would now like to give the floor to Mrs Oomen-Ruijten from the CDA.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u310	Mr. Chairman. Between the conclusion of the CETA negotiations in 2017 and today, the world order and the balance of power have changed considerably. More than ever, forces are joined. Coalitions are sought with equals and natural partners to address international challenges. Canada is clearly such a like-minded partner. Member of the G7 and the G20 and also an important partner in NATO. The EU and Canada stand side by side in a war raging on our European continent. Canada, with us, has an international tradition, a tradition of values, the pursuit of an international legal order in proven and open democracy, security and diversity. At the hearing, held in our home in June 2020, the Canadian ambassador also refers to CETA as a cornerstone for the strong and historical ties we have as the Netherlands with Canada. We must not forget. We also support, as Canada does, the great importance of a global multilateral trading system and we are committed to maintaining it. I say this so explicitly because the agreement was negotiated and also came about during the period when President Trump indicated that he would not see anything, even more so, to take an American initiative to cut off all multilateral links. That's what we were worried about. It is precisely because of the very difficult attitude in the multilateral system of the US and China, both protagonists, that the strengthening of ties with second-tier countries such as Japan, Korea, the UK and certainly Canada is so important. In today's world where there are many certainties under pressure, a truly fundamental reflection on the structure and openness of world trade is needed. This is necessary for the EU, but also for our country. Our country, which is so dependent on consultation for prosperity but certainly also for achieving the sustainability objectives, depends on perseverance and international agreements. Furthermore, we must recognise that qualitative prosperity can only be achieved if we have regulated world trade in these areas. When we talk today about CETA, a strategic partnership agreement between the EU and its Member States and Canada, in order to provide a framework for political dialogue and the development and relaxation of trade and investment, we know that this proposal could also count on a majority in the House of Representatives, which, as I have heard, will also be the case in this House. The adoption of the broad economic and trade agreement between Canada and the EU has raised more questions. I have read the report of the hearing again and I see that groups have asked questions to varying degrees. Mrs Faber of the PVV was also critical of ICS at the time. Mr Otten also said something about ICS back then. Mr Beuker, on behalf of the Democracy Forum at the time, said that he wanted to have a guarantee that American products that do not meet our European standards would not come into the EU via a back door. Mr. Chairman. The CDA Group feels responsible for the way in which we give the EU interest and therefore the Dutch interest position as an important player in the geopolitical field. Dutch exports to Canada have increased by 29%. That is twice the EU average. The trade surplus with Canada increased from 2.5 billion to 3.2 billion in that period. ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u317	Yes, Mr. Chairman. Let us first look at the power of ratification. It is explained in the position paper of Professor Kuiper that was introduced during the hearing. I would like to mention some fundamental changes. ICS does not concern private arbitration, but it is a permanent court with a first instance tribunal and an appeal tribunal. There are well-established investment protection provisions. Judges have been appointed by Contracting Parties for a fixed term. There are transparent procedures in the process. Letterbox companies are excluded from investment protection. The policy freedom of countries cannot be restricted. ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u324	Thank you, Mr President. Sometimes it's hard to pick. Some values that may be in conflict with each other will not be translated into a common denominator. Do we, for example, opt for more freedom or more equality? The choice for one is at the expense of the other. There is no neutral way to choose between the two values. A similar situation is taking place today. Do we opt for more free trade and investment, or do we opt for national legal protection and sovereignty? That is one of the essential choices we are considering today regarding the adoption of the Comprehensive Economic Trade Agreement between Canada and the European Union. The Comprehensive and Economic Trade Agreement, CETA, is the most comprehensive and ambitious trade agreement in the history of the European Union. My group welcomes the final treatment in the Upper Chamber. The position and attitude of the Netherlands towards this mixed treaty should be clear. In the run-up to this treaty after seven years of intensive consultation, resulting in 1,598 pages, a lot has been spoken and written. Hundreds of questions have already been put to Mrs Oomen and have also been answered neatly. This mixed trade agreement, with a section on trade but also a section on investment, mixed today and today in the Chamber, continues to create a great deal in society and rightly, my group believes. This morning we saw that again at a demonstration here at the entrance. This treaty between the European Union and Canada is not about our gratitude towards the Canadians. For our part, the debate is not about the desire to trade or invest in a level playing field. My group is very much in favour of this, as many in our country do. Trade is in our blood and suits us, in the past, in the present and probably, if it is JA21, also in the future. The strategic partnership with Canada is therefore not a problem for us. So what is the issue with this CETA Convention? In recent years, several treaties have been concluded by the EU. They have simply not been dealt with in the States-General and simply came to fruition. The recent trade agreement between the EU and Japan, certainly a large economy, is a good example of this. The reason for Parliament's debate lies in the fact that, for the first time, a special and newly designed dispute settlement, the so-called Investment Court System, the ICS, is linked to the aforementioned trade agreement. The investment part and the associated arbitration court, that's where the shoe is wringing. For my group, this new EU supranational dispute settlement is really unnecessary and undesirable. Investors, both domestic and foreign, have ample opportunities to defend their interests in our tried and tested system. Mr. Chairman. The trade agreement aims to provide a framework for political dialogue and the development of trade and investment between the EU and Canada. Fine! Standard clauses have been included for this purpose, for example on human rights, the fight against terrorism, but also on the spread of illegal small arms and judicial cooperation, all of which concern the European part and the Netherlands. Fine too! However, there is a considerable division over the establishment of that supranational arbitration court that works according to the ICS. That is a form of ISDS, the Investor-State Dispute Settlement, the investor-state dispute resolution, in which Canadian investors, both companies and shareholders........................................................................................................................................................................................ It is not clear to my group what the added value of this supranational arbitration court, the associated joint committee and the specialised committees that can give binding opinions in various areas. The Netherlands, like Canada, has a full legal system. Why our own legal system would not comply and why a parallel legal system for Canadian investors would be necessary has remained completely unclear and not supported by evidence for us. That is why my group considers it undesirable that a choice for companies should be built in by setting up ICS. In our view, the Dutch legal system is more than adequate to resolve the disputes between the parties, including in the framework of CETA. Why should we make our national law subordinate to the interests of investors? A Dutch judge ideally judges with in mind the general interest. A judge of the ICS only assesses the investor's interest. What, according to the Minister, is the improvement for the Netherlands? As a result, the regulatory chill effect is also a difficult word. This means that, under threat of damages claims, the legislator may decide not to take measures. The Minister and some in the Chamber deny this effect, and yet my group doubts it. We have doubts about this, because this was the very reason for Canada to withdraw ICS from the treaty with America. The Minister of Foreign Affairs, Chrystia Freeland, said: by removing ISDS from our treaty, we have "strengthened the right to take public action in the public interest, to protect public health and the environment." Moreover, the CETA Convention entered into force provisionally in 2017, without the aforementioned investment protection. The trade results also seem to be favourable. That has been mentioned several times. Investment and trade between the European Union and Canada therefore flourish without the ICS. Apart from the ICS, my group still has some constitutional objections to the ratification process of CETA. Article 112 (1) of the Constitution provides that "the judicial authority is to have jurisdiction over disputes concerning civil rights and claims'. Damage claims against the State for investment protection are civil claims that can also be called claims. Damage claims against the State must therefore be settled through our national legal system. Article 81 of the Constitution provides that "the adoption of laws shall be carried out jointly by the Government and the States-General." CETA amends Article 112 of the Constitution by transferring judicial powers to the CETA Arbitration Court on Investment. Pursuant to Article 91 (3) of the Constitution, "in the event that a treaty amends articles of the Constitution, a two-thirds majority in both chambers is required for ratification'. Finally, Mr President. Article 30.9 (2) of the CETA Convention clearly states that the provisions of Chapter 8....................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................... That is the so-called sunset clause. How desirable does the Minister consider this to be? I'd like a response. Mr. Chairman. We look forward to the answers. I'm at the end of my first term. Thank you.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u344	Mr. Chairman. I see Mr Van Ballekom leaving the Chamber as soon as I enter the speaker's seat. Apparently, he doesn't want to hear it. Mr. Chairman. The CETA Convention provides for a new tribunal, the Investment Court System, short term "ICS." That tribunal is exclusive to multinationals. This ICS Investment Tribunal, governed by Chapter 8 of the CETA Convention, is a solution for other countries that are also parties to CETA but whose legal systems are not sufficiently reliable or adequate, for example some countries in Eastern Europe. That is not because Canada's and the Netherlands' legal systems would not be good, because they are indeed bad, but to solve the problem for those participating in the CETA Convention who do not have proper independent legal protection. For example, this CETA Convention creates a legal race to the bottom. The proceedings surrounding the parliamentary treatment of this CETA Convention are symptomatic and illustrative of how the Rutte Cabinet deals with the States General, that is to say, our elected parliament. The whole parliamentary trickbox was raised to take this treaty as quickly by the First Chamber under high pressure of time on the last day of the year, after two years first by the coalition and also by the PvdA all kinds of expert meetings, hearings, expert meetings and everything else organized to buy time. At some point, our group stopped attending these meetings, because it became increasingly clear that this was only a delay for certain parties, especially the coalition, who are very upset with the six seats they lack here in the First Chamber. As a hunter spying on his prey in a hunting lodge, the minister was waiting for an unguarded moment to occur at which she could strike, and this ill-digestible CETA treaty could quickly and deviously slip through this senate. Apparently, that moment was the last day of the summer recess of the First Chamber. Most Dutch people are head of vacation after two years of corona, the House of Representatives is already recessing and most members of the parliamentary press are also working on their holiday plans. No wonder more and more people are starting to lose faith in politics. Last week, in this House, we voted in favour of six amendments to the Constitution, at second reading. This was, among other things, the constitutional right to a fair trial, which is also very relevant in relation to this CETA Convention. It was also a new preamble to our Constitution, which states that the Netherlands is a democratic constitutional state. This required the consent of a two-thirds majority of this Chamber. This is the result of the idea of popular sovereignty. The people have sovereignty, the Constitution is being changed, and the voters can then decide on that change during the elections. After the first reading in Parliament, the second reading will take place, with the newly constituted Chambers once again having to confirm these constitutional changes by two-thirds majority; otherwise that sovereignty cannot be given away, because then the people would have no control over it. This CETA Treaty, which gives up important parts of the sovereignty of the Netherlands, must therefore be adopted by the First Chamber with a two-thirds majority, is the vision of many. There is indeed much to be said for this on the basis of the Constitution. We would like to hear from Minister Schreinemacher how she intends to achieve this two-thirds majority, because only with the Labour Party will it not succeed. We have therefore co-signed Mrs Faber's motion from the PVV, which calls for this. ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u375	Mr. Chairman. Thank you to all Members for the contributions and the questions. Your Chamber is today discussing the European Union's Comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement with Canada, better known as CETA, and the Strategic Partnership Agreement which focuses on political cooperation between the EU and Canada. On behalf of my fellow Minister Hoekstra, I will also talk about this strategic partnership agreement, abbreviated SPA. Today's debate is long awaited. CETA and SPA were signed in 2016. The European Parliament approved the Treaties in February 2017. The provisional application of SPA started in April 2017 and the provisional application of CETA subsequently started in September of that year. In 2020, the House of Representatives approved the ratification of the CETA Convention and the Strategic Partnership Agreement, after which discussion started in the First Chamber. In the meantime, a large number of written questions from the members of the BDO Committee of your Chamber on CETA have been answered. Round tables have been organised and stakeholders have been spoken to. I would like to thank the members of the BDO Committee for the careful written preparation of the CETA approval bill recently. Mr. Chairman. As indicated by your Member Koole and several other Members, the current geopolitical situation calls for a mature and effective EU action. In doing so, the EU needs equal partners. Bonds are more important than ever, but they're not for picking up. Canada is in all respects a unique partner for the EU and for the Netherlands and with Canada, in addition to an intensive trade relationship, we share common interests and essential values of democratic principles, human rights, fundamental freedoms, the rule of law and good governance. And we share the conviction and commitment to our international, law-based organization and universal values. Canada is a member of the G7 and the G20. It is an active player in foreign policy matters and as a NATO ally it has been contributing to European security for decades. SPA and CETA form the basis for further deepening the relationship between the EU and Canada. Then about SPA. Let me first mention the political and security relationship with Canada, which is the subject of the strategic partnership agreement. Canada is a crucial partner for the EU. Let me give an example of some important issues. For example, Ukraine. Since 2015, Canada has had a military training mission in Ukraine, with which approximately 33,000 Ukrainian soldiers have been trained in recent years. Since the war, the Canadian military have been brought back from Ukraine. Canada has provided support in many areas since the outbreak of the war in Ukraine, including military, humanitarian, financial and cyber. Canada, together with the President of the European Commission and the Polish President, organised the "Stand up for Ukraine" global pledge event, in partnership with the international interest organisation Global Citizen. That campaign has given more than $12.4 billion a commitment. In the NATO context, Canada has currently deployed 450 military forces in Latvia as a leading nation in the context of our forward defence, i.e. the enhanced forward presence. The Netherlands and Canada are also very similar in NATO to arms control, in particular nuclear weapons control.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u389	My question concerns the order of the debate. This has nothing to do with CETA or with the Minister's portfolio. We can determine that. We're just talking about SPA right now. All these arguments therefore do not apply to CETA.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u390	I am also here on behalf of Minister Hoekstra of Foreign Affairs. This part was in particular on his behalf.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u391	No, but then that's clear. This was about SPA and so it wasn't about CETA. That's what I wanted to find out.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u392	Yes. Okay, then I'll move on. SPA and CETA are both part of the EU's instruments to strengthen our relationship with Canada, because geopolitics also means being able to connect different policy areas and the relationship with third countries from the full breadth. Political cooperation, SPA, and trade, CETA, are not separate worlds, but go hand in hand. There is no other way to do this in a partnership that is so important. Both agreements strengthen the relationship with Canada and its essential building blocks in an intensive cooperation across the full breadth of our agenda with Canada. It is with Canada that these agreements can be used to consolidate our relationship. That is urgently needed in a restless, unpredictable world. I now come to CETA. First of all, I would like to address the general geopolitical importance of this. Although the text of the CETA Convention is unchanged, the circumstances are not. Both geopolitics and the implementation of EU trade policy have changed a lot since the consent of the Lower House more than two years ago. We also have more experience with the operation and consequences of CETA itself. The vast majority of the Convention has been applied provisionally for almost five years now. Entrepreneurs are increasingly finding their way into the benefits of CETA. For example, the use of tariff preferences has increased further, unnecessary barriers such as double testing obligations have been removed and the volume of trade between Canada and the Netherlands has increased from EUR 7.2 billion in 2016 to EUR 10.3 billion in 2020. As I just said, it's a time of geopolitical turbulence. The debate on CETA is therefore very relevant in this context. Europe and Canada stand side by side in a turbulent world with a war on the edges of our continent. The Trade Treaty is a central part of our relations with an important transatlantic ally. The Trade Treaty also deals with shared values and interests between the EU and Canada, between the Netherlands and Canada, about the importance of EU cooperation with similar partners in order to continue to defend these values and interests. One could say that the importance of alliances and the establishment of agreements on international trade has become even greater. There are critics who say: at your service, that good relations with Canada, but you don't need a trade agreement for that at all? I say to them: trade and investment are the cement of good relations. You're gonna settle that with a treaty base. We see that within the EU and we see that in countries with which we conclude such treaties. With a country such as South Korea, commonality is increased by giving our trade relations by treaty a firmer basis. A country such as Vietnam is adapting to our standards now that we have agreed on them by treaty. And, as many of your Members have said, what signal do we send when we say to Canada: we do not want a trade agreement with you; we have no confidence in it? Trade agreements and CETA in particular are therefore not on their own. Especially in the current geopolitical context, trade agreements strengthen existing partnerships with countries outside the European Union. In addition, they can contribute to the diversification of trade chains and thereby reduce risky strategic dependencies of non-similar countries. Modern trade agreements go beyond trade alone. The EU and the Netherlands are at the forefront of strengthening and protecting our international organisation, which is based on legal rules and universal values. That world order is not self-evident, that's how we see it every day. From a geopolitical and geoeconomic perspective, the fact that the EU is concluding trade agreements is of great importance, for example as a counterweight to the Chinese Belt and Road Initiative, and especially as geopolitical relations shift as a result of the war in Ukraine. CETA as a modern and ambitious trade agreement also has an important role to play here. That is actually an open and strategic autonomy in practice. Modern trade agreements are also about shared values, as I have just said, and the ambitions in the areas of sustainable development, the environment and labour protection. This is the subject of concrete binding agreements in CETA. I'll come back to that later. Although some parties point out that these agreements do not go far enough, I see them as an important step forward in expanding international cooperation. If we reject a treaty because it would not go far enough, we will also say no to the steps that can be taken now. It is precisely on the basis of these steps that we can move forward and then work towards further cooperation. Before going into the content of the Convention, I would like to begin with a general statement on the trade relationship between Europe and Canada. CETA is not the beginning of trade relations, as many of your members have reminded us today. Even without this trade agreement, these like-minded partners have been trading for decades. It has also been said a number of times that CETA, as a neoliberal agreement, is aimed at maximum liberalisation, but that really is an incorrect representation of the facts. The Treaty is much more a framework within which trade is facilitated and in which we jointly strive for higher and better standards. Canada has traditionally been an important trading partner for the Netherlands. Canada is the twelfth destination of Dutch exports of goods outside the EU and the twentieth country of origin of Dutch imports from outside the EU. CETA as an ambitious and comprehensive trade agreement further strengthens the trade relationship and strengthens the regulatory framework on the basis of which this trade takes place. CETA, as has been said, has been provisionally applied for the most part for almost five years. Trade between the EU and Canada has become easier at that time. Obstacles are and are removed. For example, unnecessary double testing on product standards will be abolished, without affecting the product standards that apply here and the product standards that apply in Canada. In particular, SMEs exporters benefit from this. Import tariffs are also going down. CETA removes 98% of all Canadian import tariffs from the EU. This means that Dutch exporters to Canada become more competitive and leave more margin on their products. For example, Canadian import tariffs for Dutch cheese have fallen from 245% to less than 1% under CETA. Conversely, Dutch importers can more easily obtain products from Canada that strengthen the competitive position of Dutch processors. As I just mentioned, the volume of our trade with Canada has increased sharply since 2016, by some EUR 3.1 billion. As we know, Dutch prosperity and employment depend heavily on our international presence. But the agreement not only increases market access over and over again. It brings together two major transatlantic partners in intensive discussions on the design of trade, on investment, on value chains and on the sustainability goals for our shared future. Some of you have raised questions and expressed concerns about certain aspects of this Convention, such as product and production standards, the powers of the Committees under CETA and the ICS in relation to the Constitution. And there are concerns that foreign investors and large companies would have a priority position vis-à-vis NGOs if complaints are made about compliance with this Convention. I'd like to answer the questions now. I would like to point out that, while CETA does not solve all the problems in the world, it provides us with a more solid basis for increasing our common prosperity, for protecting our public interests, for protecting vulnerable sectors in our agriculture and for substantial progress in transatlantic cooperation on sustainability objectives. I would also like to say that CETA, together with SPA, is in favour of the international cooperation and partnership that the EU needs at this time more than ever.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u393	Thank you. That was your block in general, I suppose.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u394	Yeah, there's something else coming up on the numbers. If I may put that under the block in general...
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u395	Go ahead.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u396	Then I'm gonna go into some questions, too. I come to examples of entrepreneurs trading with Canada. Much has been said that it is only a treaty for the multinationals. That's incorrect. There are currently more than 4,000 Dutch companies trading with Canada, most of which are SMEs, "CETA in your town," as the European Commission says. This concerns, for example, a trader in tap equipment and materials for beverages. There is also a company that provides a platform with geomarketing tools. Another company is developing products for sustainable climate control in buildings. So it's not just big multinationals that benefit from CETA, but also a lot of SMEs. In reply to Mr Backer's question, who wants the official figures of the export balance and wants to hear which sectors benefit from it, so far known to the government and the European Commission, I say the following. In 2021, Dutch goods imports from Canada amounted to 2.4 billion euros, while the Netherlands exported 3.4 billion euros of goods. For the year 2021, no figures for trade in services are yet available, but for the year 2020. In 2020, imports of Canadian services amounted to 1.6 billion euros, while Dutch services exported 3.2 billion euros. I would also like to share the trade figures between the EU and Canada. In 2021, EU goods imports from Canada amounted to 23.6 billion euros, while the EU exported 37.3 billion euros of goods. In 2020, imports of Canadian services amounted to EUR 11.7 billion, while the EU exported EUR 15.5 billion. Both imports and exports are important for the economy. Mr Backer specifically asked about the trade balance. I can tell you that in 2020 both the EU and the Netherlands had a trade surplus of both goods and services. Then Mr Van Apeldoorn, on behalf of the SP, asked for progress on the path of hyperglobalisation and the role that CETA would play in it. I do not share Mr Van Apeldoorn's view of CETA as an instrument for promoting hyperglobalisation. CETA, on the contrary, provides a framework to strengthen predictable and rules-based trade. In doing so, facilitating trade is an objective, but also, for example, sustainable development. Just now, trade agreements are valuable. They can contribute to the diversification of trading partners, as I have just said, and address undesirable dependencies. CETA is also about shared values and interests between the EU, and certainly not undermining our democracy, as I will explain later. On behalf of the Animal Party, it was asked: which consumer goods will be cheaper by CETA and why does the government want to promote their consumption? Lower import rates for certain goods or parts thereof will make products directly or indirectly cheaper for consumers. Two specific products whose imports increased significantly after CETA provided lower import rates are cranberries and sweet potatoes. Under CETA, the import tariff on cranberry and cranberry juice imports was reduced from around 17% to 0%. During that period, since the provisional application of CETA, imports of cranberry and cranberry juice increased from EUR 13.7 million in 2016 to EUR 39.9 million in 2021. The same applies to sweet potatoes. As a result of CETA, the import tariff was reduced from around 8% to 0% and imports increased from EUR 7.2 million in 2016 to EUR 16.8 million in 2021. The purpose of the Cabinet is not to promote the consumption of specific goods through CETA, but to remove trade barriers in total. Then Mrs Huizinga of the Christian Union asked me how to assess the values of CETA for our economy so far and whether I have up-to-date data on the impact of CETA on our Dutch economy. CETA gives Dutch companies new or better access to a market of 35 million people. This is of great value to the Netherlands, which, as a trading country, earns a third of its GDP with international trade. This will, among other things, make it easier for Dutch companies to export products to Canada and to use products from Canada more easily in their production processes. They improve their international competitiveness, and this has a positive impact on our economy. The agreement has been provisionally applied since 2017. It is therefore difficult to determine exactly what the impact of CETA on our GDP is. The increased trade since the provisional application of CETA already shows that Dutch market players are taking advantage of the economic opportunities that CETA offers. From 2016 to 2020, exports to Canada increased from EUR 4.9 billion to EUR 6.5 billion and imports from EUR 2.3 billion to EUR 3.8 billion. Research by the CBS shows that our exporters and importers are increasingly making better use of CETA's tariff preferences. In 2020 54% of Dutch exports benefited from tariff preferences under CETA. In 2018, that was 38%, so it's rising. Mrs Huizinga also asked if I could go into the information provided by evofenedex and VNO-NCW that Dutch SMEs in particular benefit from this trade agreement, even much more than multinationals. As I said earlier, I can generally agree that Dutch SMEs can benefit more from CETA than multinationals. Research carried out by the CBS shows that independent SMEs use tariff preferences in CETA relatively more often than large companies. It is precisely the cost savings through low rates that are of great value to SMEs. Well, then I'm at the end of the general heading.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u413	Okay. Mrs Prast also said that the cabinet makes demands on our own farmers that are not made of Canadian farmers and that that is unfair competition for Dutch farmers. I do not agree with that, because for the use of plant protection products, the EU and countries outside the EU have their own laws and regulations to assess whether or not plant protection products should be placed on that market. This assessment may vary from country to country, as countries take account of their own agricultural, climatic, environmental and geographical conditions. As a result, plant protection products other than those in the EU may be on the market in countries outside the EU. If use is authorised in a third country, an interested party in that country has the possibility to apply to the Commission for a higher MRL, a so-called import tolerance, for import into the EU. However, the Commission's decision on granting a higher MRL is independent of CETA and generally applies to placing products on the EU market from all third countries. An increased import tolerance shall only be granted if the risk assessment shows that food and feed containing this higher MRL are safe for human and animal consumption. Then Mr Van Dijk's question of the SGP whether the Netherlands makes higher demands on animal welfare and can therefore make Canadian competitors cheaper. Agricultural entrepreneurs have to meet different standards, which vary from country to country. Eventually, there are a multitude of factors involved in determining the final competitive position of Canadian and Dutch companies, as I said earlier. It is therefore a question of capital costs, such as land and buildings, energy input, energy costs, taxes and labour rights. So there are several factors that are part of your entire competitive position. Then Mrs Huizinga of the Christian Union. She asked me if, looking back on the past five years, concerns about inferior producers, for example, were justified. These concerns have not been justified. NVWA and Customs carry out random checks on imports of products into the EU. If products do not comply with EU rules at border controls, they will be refused. Since 2017, 38 out of a total of 4,502 shipments from Canada have been refused by NVWA, i.e. 0.8%. This mainly concerned refusals of documents and omissions in material control, e.g. incorrect packaging or exposure to too high temperatures. This is a lower percentage than the total number of refusals on imports from third countries in 2020/2021. Mrs Huizinga of the Christian Union also asked about competition and the problem of nitrogen. Can our farmers be more exposed to competition from Canada due to the problem of nitrogen, because there are no restrictions on agriculture because of nitrogen? The rules in force in Canada are different from those in Europe and the Netherlands, but that does not mean that the level of protection of humans, the environment and animals is necessarily lower or higher. The circumstances in Canada are also different, as I said earlier. The need for agricultural entrepreneurs to comply with various national standards that differ applies in various areas. Ultimately, a multitude of factors play a role in determining this ultimate competitive position, and sustainability must not be priced out of the market, but must be priced in the market. This applies to all aspects of sustainability, from animal welfare requirements to nitrogen measures. For export, the Netherlands is therefore also committed to the export of high-quality, sustainable products at CETA rather than focusing on large volumes. CETA gives Dutch farmers new or better access to a market of 35 million people, and this applies not only to agricultural goods and agri-food products, but also, for example, to agricultural-related machines and knowledge. The Dutch agricultural sector, and in particular the dairy and meat producers, therefore has a trade surplus with Canada. Mr Van Dijk of the SGP asked how the government sees nitrogen policy, i.e. the competitive disadvantage and future benefits for agriculture at CETA. In fact, I have already answered this question. For agricultural products sensitive to competition from third countries and subject to a quota under CETA, the balance of trade with Canada is developing positively. For example, the Netherlands exports more pork and beef to Canada than it imports. This positive trade balance for meat has grown since the provisional application of CETA in 2017. So much for the agricultural cube.
