ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u468	Mr. Chairman. Several members of your Chamber, including member Karimi, have doubts about CETA's contribution to sustainability and the agreements contained in the agreement. The trade and investment agreement between Canada and the EU is a good example of what sustainability chapters can deliver. For example, Canada ratified the ILO Convention on Collective Labour Negotiation in 2017. In addition, the Parties have extended the scope of their agreements on trade and sustainable development. The Joint Committee adopted two joint recommendations in the field of trade and gender and trade and climate in 2018. Under the sustainability agreements in CETA, Canada and the EU will organise a cleantech meeting this year to explore how CETA can further contribute to the green transition in the EU and Canada. A high level of ambition for sustainability goes hand in hand with good compliance with commitments made. Some of you have asked questions about this too. I would like to emphasise that the sustainability provisions, like the other provisions in CETA, are legally binding on all contracting parties. The EU and Canada are constantly discussing the implementation of these provisions. I would like to emphasise that Canada complies with the commitments made. Like the EU, Canada is a champion of climate policy and effective implementation of the Paris Agreement and other multilateral environmental agreements. The EU and Canada are also committed to the effective implementation of fundamental ILO conventions on labour law. Canada, as previously indicated by former Trade Commissioner Hogan, has also ratified two ILO conventions in recent years, one on collective action, as I said, and one on the minimum age for access to employment. Finally, in line with the sustainability agreements, the EU and Canada share best practices in the areas of gender, imvo and climate. Mr Backer of D66 asked whether any abuses of the type that some civil society parties warned about have materialised. Product standards are subject to EU rules in the case of imports to the EU. This applies to food safety but also, for example, to how a device must be plugged in. This is not under discussion in trade agreements, including CETA. That's something that's being monitored. For production standards, i.e. the method of production, each country produces in its own way. It is largely determined by the local context. But it is precisely in a trade agreement, and thus in CETA, that agreements are made on trade and sustainable development. In doing so, the EU and Canada commit themselves to international labour and environmental standards, i.e. to standards that partly determine the method of production. In CETA, the Contracting Parties confirm that they will comply with fundamental conventions of the International Labour Organisation, the ILO. They also commit themselves to the effective implementation of multilateral climate and environmental agreements to which they are party, including the Paris Agreement. In addition, CETA confirms the right of governments to regulate. I'll come back to that later. There has been no violation of these agreements. No, the EU and Canada are working hard on this. Mr Van Apeldoorn of the SP asked about the environment and animal welfare. I was just talking about that. I am going to speak again about animal welfare, but let me stress it again. There are also social discussions in Canada about improving animal welfare and the environment. Canada follows a different path from the EU in the development of animal welfare and environmental rules. Their regulations are different, but CETA offers a platform to continue working together on animal welfare. I've just said the rest. Mr Van Apeldoorn also asked whether not only product requirements, but also production requirements are important. There is also a lot of discussion on this at European level. So we're also looking to the future. The EU has made several proposals for measures in the area of production standards. These EU proposals all follow their own path. For example, it is a proposal for a regulation on deforestation-free products or the Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism. In doing so, it is important that the measure contributes to the objective set and that non-discrimination principles are respected in order to avoid unjustified distortion of trade. We are also bound by WTO rules. Differences in regulation of production do not necessarily lead to lower standards or unfair competition, as I have just said. That's what we need to handle case by case. Mr Koole asked about the enforcement of these agreements. Through a dialogue, Contracting Parties monitor compliance with these agreements and identify opportunities and problems in the field of trade and sustainable development. Civil society can contribute to this through so-called national advisory groups. CETA provides for a dispute resolution for alleged breaches of sustainability agreements. This mechanism consists of the possibility of entering into formal consultations which, if the consultations do not lead to a solution, an independent panel of experts may eventually draw up a final report with recommendations. The Contracting Parties are obliged to take all measures necessary to implement this final report of the Expert Panel. The Committee on Trade and Sustainable Development shall monitor its follow-up. The expert panel report shall also be published. Mr Koole also asked about the review of the TSD chapters, the sustainability chapters, in trade agreements and the power of trade partnerships, the Commission's communication containing recommendations to strengthen sustainability agreements. He asked whether I could undertake to urge the Commission to introduce these action points as soon as possible. The cabinet welcomes the Commission's communication and supports the efforts to contribute to sustainable development through trade policy. Indeed, some of the Action Lines aim at improving compliance and monitoring of sustainability agreements and therefore apply to existing trade agreements such as CETA. These Action Lines do not require treaty changes. The Cabinet may urge the Commission to follow up on the action lines for the implementation and monitoring of sustainability agreements as soon as possible. After the summer recess, the Chamber will be informed, as we always do, by means of a BNC-fiche about the government's juice interpretation of the communication. Mrs Prast asked whether we could not negotiate after the ratification of CETA, i.e. with the improvements to the complaints mechanism. She asked if I agree with her that these improvements cannot remove the asymmetry between investor protection and other stakeholders. Commissioner Dombrovskis has indeed promised a number of improvements to the Complaints Mechanism Directive. These were introduced by the European Commission on 22 June. It is not necessary to introduce these improvements to the complaint mechanism in order to renegotiate CETA including ICS, because the complaint mechanism on the EU side, where alleged lack of compliance with existing trade agreements can be identified, is already operational and applies to all conventions. The comparison between investment protection and access for trade unions, NGOs and citizens to enforce sustainability agreements is not going to work. But I'll be on ICS later. Then there is Mrs Oomen's question as to whether I would like to monitor and undertake that Canada's and the Commission's promise to include new points in the TSD Review in CETA will be fulfilled in its review. Yes, the Netherlands can use within the Council to swiftly implement the action lines set out in the Commission's Communication on improved compliance and monitoring of existing agreements in trade agreements such as CETA. The Council has yet to reach an opinion on policies that require impact in treaty agreements. At the cabinet position we come back to a BNC chip, as I just said. So we'll have that discussion a little later. But at least we have now strongly insisted on the complaints mechanism. I will come back to that later when it comes to the mechanism. I am not saying too much about this now, but I can imagine that we, as a cabinet, are in favour of it. This was the end of this block.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u487	Mr. Chairman. The grievance mechanism, aka the Single Entry Point. Several members of your Chamber have expressed doubts about the monitoring and enforcement of sustainability agreements in this Convention, particularly in this area, in EU trade agreements. We just talked about that. Important steps have been taken in recent years. The appointment of the Chief Trade Enforcement Officer in 2020 symbolises the increased commitment to the implementation of trade agreements. With the Chief Trade Enforcement Officer, compliance with EU trade agreements has a face, both towards third countries and internally within the EU. Within the European Commission, the appointment has been accompanied by a reorganisation and mobilisation of people and resources. In order to further strengthen the monitoring and enforcement of trade agreements and to give a clear role to European civil society, the European Commission launched on 16 November 2020 a complaint mechanism, i.e. the Single Entry Point, where NGOs, businesses and trade unions may raise alleged lack of compliance with trade agreements with the Commission. It is therefore about agreements on market access and sustainability under all trade agreements that the EU has, not only CETA. Companies that want to address market access problems therefore have exactly the same status and access as NGOs or trade unions that want to address sustainability issues under this mechanism. Your Chamber has played an important role with civil society in establishing and further developing this complaint mechanism. The establishment of the complaint mechanism followed an exchange of views between your BDO committee and the then Commissioner Hogan. Then, on the basis of your input and that of civil society, my predecessors and I asked the Commission to review the directives of the mechanism, and successfully, because the Commission has further clarified what information it needs from complainants to deal with a complaint and what the Commission's handling process looks like. After having had some hesitation and having been urged by your Chamber through me, on the basis of written questions received from your Chamber on Tuesday 7 June, the Commission finally introduced the timetables for dealing with complaints about sustainability agreements on 22 June last. In doing so, the European Commission has honoured an important wish of the Netherlands and, in particular, of your Chamber. In this third round of written questions, your members also asked questions about what the complaints mechanism actually changes to CETA. This question has also been exchanged several times between your members. Let me be clear about this: the substance of the complaint mechanism does not change the CETA Convention. However, it does provide an input for trade unions, NGOs and companies to bring their concerns or complaints about inadequate compliance with trade agreements, including CETA, directly to the Commission. In this way, the complaint mechanism contributes to better monitoring and enforcement of the arrangements in CETA. A frequently mentioned point is the alleged unbalanced approach in trade and investment agreements between international investors on the one hand and trade unions and NGOs on the other. The Cabinet does not share this view. The protection of investment in this area will be further enhanced as additional legal protection for foreign investors who, for example, have to deal with discriminatory regulation without complying with procedures for good governance. These are situations where their cross-border investment is directly at risk. At the same time, this legal protection does not have the scope that is suggested; investment protection controls specific, closely defined situations. I shall come back to the improvements regarding investment protection in CETA. On the other hand, the agreements in trade agreements on both market access and sustainability concern wider agreements between the EU and third countries, with the parties to the agreements, i.e. state actors, in order to address a lack of compliance. Both companies and NGOs can call on their own government to ensure that the other contracting party complies with the agreements. The new complaints mechanism allows companies, trade unions and NGOs to make such a call to the European Commission in a transparent and unambiguous manner. Then Mr Koole, Mrs Prast and Mr Van Apeldoorn asked if the Single Entry Point changed anything about CETA and nothing about ICS. Indeed, as I said, the complaints mechanism does not change the EU's and Canada's commitments in CETA. That was not the intention of setting up the complaints mechanism. In fact, it applies to all trade agreements. The complaint mechanism thus provides an entrance to all trade agreements for companies, trade unions and NGOs to highlight their concerns or complaints about compliance with the agreements made in EU trade agreements including CETA. It is then up to the Commission to follow up an alleged breach of agreements and to contribute to better monitoring and enforcement of the agreements in CETA. Mrs Prast asked about the asymmetry between the protection of investors and that of other stakeholders. As I said earlier, I will come to that later on, under CETA, the ICS deals with the mutual rights and obligations of investors and governments with an investment and lays down safeguards as we know them in Dutch law. It is therefore a question of protection in specific, closely defined situations. The comparison with broader agreements in trade agreements does not apply. Indeed, the complaint mechanism does not change the agreements in CETA, including the ICS. So I didn't mean to. As has been said, it is up to the Commission to follow up the alleged breach of agreements.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u488	Mr Otten is already eager to make an interruption.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u489	The reason I was eager was because I heard the Minister say very clearly: the complaint mechanism does not change the CETA Convention at all. That is exactly what I said to Mr Koole, who is so pleased with his dead sparrow of the complaints mechanism. That is the reason for him to vote in favour. ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u490	You can well hear me say that the complaint mechanism does not change the content of the CETA Convention, but at the entrance it offers to NGOs, businesses and trade unions to express their concerns and to encourage the Commission to initiate proceedings. Actually, you start a conversation with your treaty party first, but then you can end up in a procedure and a panel of experts.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u492	I'll take a look around. I don't see anyone... Yes, Mrs. Oomen.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u493	Mr President, I would like to say a few more words. Could the Minister explain again why this change in the complaint mechanism is so important? I think that is a good thing, because it is clearly not clear to everyone and is not understood by everyone immediately.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u494	Thank you for this question, Mrs Oomen. The bottom line is that companies in particular initially had access to that complaint mechanism. This was about complaints about the market access that was regulated in CETA. NGOs and trade unions couldn't use that grievance mechanism, so they were a little empty-handed. It is precisely through the broadening of the complaint mechanism that NGOs and trade unions now have access to the complaint mechanism and can encourage the European Commission to go further and address their opposite party, their party to the treaty, whether it is about access or sustainability agreements. Thanks to your Chamber, therefore, the Commission has also linked timelines to this. Within a given time limit, an NGO or a complainant must therefore hear whether the complaint is provided with all the necessary information, whether the complaint will be brought forward, or whether further information is needed, etc. In this way they also have more certainty.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u496	I would ask Members to take their seats and welcome everyone back. The next item is the continuation of the debate on CETA and the Strategic Partnership Agreement. I give the floor to the Minister for Foreign Trade and Development Cooperation. I think we were ready to deal with the issue of investment protection. You have the floor.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u497	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Investment protection. The most widely spoken and written about the Investment Court System may have been related to CETA. I would first like to take a step back and talk about the purpose of investment treaties. An investment treaty is first and foremost an instrument that promotes private investment in countries with a less robust governance system: private, foreign investment that is an important tool for achieving the SDGs. This is often about large and long-term investment in the infrastructure of these countries. If investors in these countries are not given independent protection against unlawful government action, the risk and thus the price increase. The construction of necessary infrastructure will then be more expensive or will not be achieved. Now a fair question is why we need an Investment Court System in the agreement with Canada, as suggested by, among other things, Mr Karimi. This is a treaty with a country that has a highly developed and independent legal system, as pointed out by my predecessors. For the Netherlands this was not necessarily necessary either. However, in the EU we are not only in charge. However, we have taken the opportunity to radically reform the old Investor-State Dispute Settlement system. Letterbox companies are excluded and transparency becomes the standard and promotes independent and impartial dispute resolution. In the areas where this is necessary, ICS offers public-law transparency and independence in a hitherto private and protected legal process. Today, several speakers pointed out that the ICS did not take any important steps towards ISDS. I find it difficult to say how the reforms I have just explained should then be qualified. Because with the ICS, we are even setting a standard that will have an impact far beyond this treaty and is the blueprint for a future multilateral investment court. Then over the right to regulate, also something often called in one breath with investment protection. That is the right to regulate legislation. I would like to make it very clear here that the freedom for governments, for the Empire, the provinces and the municipalities to regulate is guaranteed in the general interest. ICS is not a vehicle for multinationals to stop climate legislation, just to take away a concern that we have heard before today. The parties reaffirmed in an interpretative statement that governments retain the right to adjust their regulations, even if this may have negative consequences for investors. The Declaration states that the Convention does not affect the ability of the Parties to achieve legitimate public policy objectives, such as the protection and promotion of public health, social services, public education, safety and the environment. As the Council of State concludes in its opinion, the interpretative statement in this way does not question that CETA does not prejudice all the legitimate objectives of public policy. The Council of State has also clearly concluded that the agreements on investment protection in the CETA Treaty itself are in conformity with the Dutch Constitution. CETA also does not create new material rights for investors. Indeed, the rules on investment protection under CETA do not differ from the functioning of the general principles of good governance under Dutch administrative law. If a co-government adheres to these rules for government transactions laid down in Dutch law, it does not have to worry about a successful claim under CETA. Even without CETA, foreign investors can rely on the general principles of good governance against the Dutch government. Scientific research, among others by Leiden University and the University of Halle, has also shown that there is no regulatory chill in the Netherlands for fear of claims from investment treaties, which several of your members spoke about today. If I turn all this off against CETA, which is creating a higher entry threshold for claims, I cannot reconcile this with the ghost stories about North American megaclaims. This was in any case also the response to a question by Mr Van Apeldoorn on regulatory chill. In addition, Mr Otten asked why ICS is needed if it is not going to be used by everyone. Firstly, it is not the government's belief that the ICS will never be used. An investor must have access to justice if he considers that he or she has infringed his or her rights. However, the cabinet believes that if the government adheres to the principles of good governance in government action, we need not be afraid that any claim will be successful. In promoting cross-border investment, it is important to ensure the legal protection of cross-border investments. In addition, this is an important start-up for a joint EU-Canada commitment to multilateral reform of investment protection dispute resolution mechanisms. Mr Van Rooijen asked whether there was indeed no transfer of sovereignty at the ICS. CETA and its ICS are not at the expense of Dutch sovereignty. There is an important distinction between ICS and the national legal system, with these two forms of dispute resolution also not mutually exclusive, as the ICS confines itself to the interpretation and application of CETA. The ICS Court should also not judge the interpretation of laws and regulations of the Netherlands and the EU on the basis of CETA. In doing so, the ICS Court can only award financial compensation and/or order the restitution of property. In the latter case, the defendant State may decide to pay financial compensation instead. The ruling does not affect national laws and regulations and therefore does not affect Dutch sovereignty. Mr Van Rooijen also asked whether a Dutch company could choose between the public court in an EU Member State and the ICS under the Trade Treaty. CETA looks, on the one hand, at EU investment in Canada and, on the other hand, at Canadian investment in the EU. A Dutch company with investments in Canada qualifying under CETA has the possibility to bring a case against the Canadian government. This can be done by both the ICS and the national courts in Canada. So the company can choose. However, in order to prevent forum shopping, under the ICS it is not possible to bring a case in both courts at the same time. Mrs Huizinga asked whether the ICS could grant a claim and whether it would affect the rules and laws that a national government is issuing. Unlike the national court, which in exceptional cases may decide to terminate a rule, the court may, if it grants a claim to the investor, only grant financial compensation and/or order the restitution of property. In the latter case, the judgment provides that the defendant may, instead, pay financial compensation in accordance with the market value prior to the expropriation. The financial compensation shall not exceed the damage suffered. The court cannot therefore award punitive damages and the judgment does not affect national laws and regulations. Mrs Prast asked if we could rule out companies going to the ICS because of Dutch laws and regulations. Under the investment protection rules in CETA, Canadian investors can go to the ICS if they believe that they are disadvantaged by Dutch government trading. The same possibility also exists under Dutch and European law and therefore no new material rights are created with the ICS. Mr Backer asked me if we could come to a multilateral permanent court step by step through the ICS. The ICS is an important step towards the achievement of a multilateral permanent court. The permanent nature and characteristics of the ICS serve as a model for the eventual establishment of a multilateral permanent court. If the ICS were not to be established now, there is very little chance that a multilateral permanent court will be established. If ICS gets there, we'll be one step closer. For this reason, the government also attaches great importance to the ICS. Then Mrs Prast asked how financial supervisors look at CETA and ICS, and what effects CETA and ICS can have on the financial sector, for example in the area of a takeover requiring a licence from DNB. When agreements are concluded, there is always wide consultation within and outside the government. In CETA, agreements have been made on financial services in a separate chapter. This concerns, for example, agreements that parties do not take discriminatory measures with regard to financial institutions and transparent regulation. In general, financial institutions must comply with applicable laws and regulations at all times. CETA shall be without prejudice to this. Unless there is a breach of the investment protection agreements laid down in CETA, the mere fact that a licence is not obtained cannot lead to a successful claim to the ICS. Then Mrs Prast also asked whether Canadian financial investors would file claims if financial supervisory measures were introduced that would affect their return. If a Canadian investor in a financial sector considers that he or she has been unlawfully treated in respect of his or her investment in the EU on the basis of financial supervisory measures, he or she may refer an alleged breach of CETA to the ICS Court. Again, CETA reaffirms and explicates the right of governments to adopt laws and regulations in order to achieve legitimate policy objectives. The mere fact that a government adopts measures that have a negative impact on an investment or intervention in an investor's profit expectations is not in itself a breach of the obligations under the Treaty. In addition, CETA explicitly states that in the case of prudential measures aimed at restructuring public debt, no claim can be made to the ICS if the restructuring is a negotiated restructuring or is imminent at the time of the submission of the claim. The core is always: only if there is an investment across the border and if the Dutch government is guilty of unfair action as described in the treaty can there be a successful claim. Mrs Huizinga mentioned that the treaty leaves room for the adoption of laws that harm investors but serve the public interest. She asked where the final consideration lies whether a law serves the public interest. Whether or not a measure serves the public interest is ultimately subject to judicial review. It follows from the Court's opinion that, subject to the provisions of CETA, the judge will review whether measures adopted in a democratic manner to protect legitimate policy objectives of public interest. In doing so, the provision in CETA is of importance which guarantees the right of public authorities to adopt laws and regulations to achieve legitimate policy objectives such as the protection of public health, safety, the environment, public morality, social protection or consumer protection or the promotion and protection of cultural diversity. It clarifies that the fact that a government adopts measures that have a negative impact on an investment or interfere with an investor's expectations does not in itself constitute a breach of the CETA obligations. The discretion of the ICS Court does not go so far as to prejudice democratically established measures to protect public interests. Ms Prast asked if a Canadian investor on a local housing market in the Netherlands could go to the ICS after new restrictions by a local government and file a claim. The court is, of course, free to proceed if it considers that it has been dealt with unlawfully. That is at the basis of our rule of law. The judge or the arbitration tribunal will give an opinion on this matter. Therefore, Canadian investors in the local housing market in the Netherlands are free to submit a claim to the ICS Court regarding an alleged breach of the CETA Convention. This is not excluded under CETA. However, CETA reaffirms and clarifies the right of governments to adopt laws and regulations in order to achieve legitimate and policy objectives. The mere fact that a government, whether local or national, adopts measures that have a negative impact on an investment or interfere with the profit expectations, in itself, as I said, does not constitute a breach of the obligations under the Treaty. So it must be a violation of the basic guarantees contained in the CETA text itself. However, I would like to reiterate that the rules on investment protection under CETA do not differ from the functioning of the general principles of good governance under Dutch administrative law. Then Mr Van Apeldoorn asked whether there is a court for workers and disadvantaged citizens. The ICS under CETA deals with the mutual rights and obligations of investors and governments regarding an investment and lays down safeguards as we know them in Dutch law. This is a legal process in the case of a government that does not comply with the basic principles of good governance. As usual in investment agreements, CETA does not include the possibility for third parties themselves to initiate proceedings against an investor. If these parties consider that they are disadvantaged in their rights, the national legal process is open under national law, in which the safeguards, such as international conventions, such as labour law, have been implemented. The CETA Convention allows third parties to join in proceedings between an investor and a State, as amicus curiae. Citizens, trade unions and NGOs who have an interest in a procedure can thus express their views and interests in the dispute directly to the Tribunal. Trade union rights are guaranteed multilaterally by conventions of the International Labour Organisation, the ILO. The ratification and implementation of these ILO conventions by governments is monitored by various committees, including trade unions. In trade agreements such as CETA, the EU agrees with third countries to ratify and implement ILO conventions, including trade union rights, in a legally binding and enforceable manner. Under the complaint mechanism, which we have already discussed this evening, the Single Entry Point, NGOs, trade unions, citizens and businesses can raise alleged lack of compliance with trade agreements with the Commission. Mr Van Dijk of the SGP asked about the evaluation of the functioning of the ICS in the CETA Convention, no later than five years after its entry into force; something the Lower House also asked for. The ICS is excluded from the provisional application of the Convention and will therefore not enter into force until it has been ratified, when CETA will also enter into force. Before CETA enters into force, it must be ratified by all Member States, the EU and Canada. Only five years later will the motion be able to be carried out. Mr Backer asked whether it was true that Canada is proposing five arbitrators for the ICS and the EU as well. A total of 15 Judges shall be appointed for the composition of the court. Five judges are nationals of Canada, five are nationals of an EU Member State and five are nationals of third countries. Three judges will sit per case: one from Canada, one from the EU and one from a third country. The arbitrator from the third country shall be chairman. Mr Backer also asked whether it was true that the Netherlands has 90 bilateral agreements with arbitration. There are currently 66 bilateral investment agreements with arbitration in force. The number has decreased, because there were investment protection agreements between Member States which no longer apply. Mr Koole asked if the cabinet was prepared to support Germany's initiatives to limit the improper use of investment protection by companies. Like Germany, the Netherlands is in favour of combating the misuse of investment protection. A concrete example of this in CETA is the exclusion of letterbox companies. The Netherlands will in principle welcome new initiatives by Germany or other treaty countries to combat improper use, but of course the Netherlands will assess this independently per proposed initiative. Then Mr Beuker asked how desirable a twenty-year sunset clause would be in Article 30 (2). Such a sunset clause is common in investment agreements, because it concerns large and long-term investments, for example in infrastructure. Although twenty years seem to be long, such a period is desirable. It is also included in Article 30 (9) (2) but that aside. Investment protection is aimed at all sectors of our economy, and it is precisely this type of private sector investment that is needed for a transition to a sustainable economy. Finally, the protection afforded by the sunset clause under CETA can only be invoked through investments made for the eventual termination of the agreement. Then Mr Van Ballekom asked whether it was true that hardly any claims were made against the Netherlands. Under the Energy Charter, two claims were filed against the Netherlands; by Uniper and by RWE. Furthermore, there are currently no claims against the Netherlands. Ms Karimi asked if the Netherlands was willing to commit itself to reforming the Energy Charter. In recent years, the Netherlands has been committed to modernising the ECT at EU level. Those modernisation negotiations were concluded on 24 June. The Netherlands and the other Member States are currently analysing that outcome and will take a position in the coming period. Moreover, CETA and the ECT are other treaties, because CETA is a comprehensive trade agreement and ECT is specifically focused on the energy sector and also contains ISDS-old style. Mr Van Apeldoorn of the SP stated that CETA would be a fossil treaty and asked if it was of this time. How does it relate to the climate objectives of this cabinet? CETA is a trade agreement and investment protection is an essential part of this. The cabinet is not in favour of excluding investors in certain sectors, such as energy, from protection. In these cases, too, the principles of good governance apply and the public authorities should not discriminate or otherwise treat companies unlawfully. CETA offers the basic conditions for this, but this does not affect the commitment of the Netherlands and the EU towards the energy transition and the climate goals set by the Dutch government. In the coalition agreement, we have also taken a broader view of the fact that we are engaged in greening our trade instruments, for example by promoting sustainable trade missions. We are also working on greening export credit insurance. So much for the ICS.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u517	Then I come to the various committees and the governance of CETA. Several speakers have expressed their concerns about this today, including the democratic nature of these committees. I would like to make it clear immediately: the powers of these committees are limited. They focus in particular on what is necessary for the proper implementation of the Convention. When decisions are taken by these committees, a decision-making procedure always applies on the European side. That's what we exchanged earlier tonight. It means that the Council decides on this and that the Netherlands as a member of the Council has a vote. The decisions of the Council shall be published in the Official Journal of the EU, which shall be accessible to all on-line. The Committees cannot therefore make any changes to the Convention itself. Only in some cases, when it comes to technical, implementing aspects, i.e. not quotas, can certain protocols and annexes to CETA be adapted. For example, we are talking about deleting or adding a geographical indication that has become obsolete. This is also the case with other trade agreements, for example with South Korea or Japan. It confers similar powers on the executive committees. This will enable the parties to keep up with the Convention and respond to new developments. Mr Backer asked whether there were reports of the Joint Committee established by the Convention and whether fundamental changes could be made to the Convention itself. Well, you can't. There are reports from the Joint Committee. All the agendas and reports of the meetings of the Joint Committee and the specialised committees are published on the European Commission's website and are therefore accessible to all. The Joint Committee may therefore decide to make technical amendments to the Protocols and a number of annexes to the Agreement. As regards amendments to the CETA Convention itself, once it has entered into force, they may be in accordance with the usual Treaty amendment procedures in accordance with Article 30.2 of the Convention. Mr Van Apeldoorn asked about the responsibility of committees and whether they were implementing legislative powers, with no democratic control. The powers of these committees are really very limited and cannot make any changes to the main text of the Convention, only on technical and implementing aspects. Prior to decision-making, the Council, acting on a proposal from the European Commission, shall adopt the position to be taken on behalf of the Union. Only on this basis can anything be agreed in the Committee on behalf of the EU. All European Member States are represented in the Council, including the Netherlands. That is why we also have a say in the decision-making process. It is therefore published in the Official Journal and all reports of the Joint Committee are public. In recent years, the European Commission has expanded the reporting of the committees under trade agreements. ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u543	As far as the technical elaboration of the Convention is concerned, the Joint Committee can do so. The Council will then have to agree to that. Those are the Member States. It is true that I can, of course, be held accountable in Parliament if the Council has taken a decision which the Chamber does not agree with.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u544	Mr Otten, very briefly.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u545	Perhaps the Minister could come back to the question of how, in her view, the Council could still influence it. That's what I like to hear.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u546	Fine.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u547	We now have to deal with the final problem: the constitutional aspects.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u548	In any event, Mr Van Apeldoorn's question was how the ICS relates to Article 112 of the Constitution. That article of the Constitution looks at the internal competence regime within the national legal sphere. This provides a constitutional guarantee that there is always a form for the trial of disputes relating to private law matters and claims. This article of the Constitution does not therefore refer to the case law of an international court. The possibility for individuals or legal persons to have recourse to dispute settlement vis-à-vis the Dutch State under international law is not new. Think of the dozens of bilateral investment protection agreements to which the Netherlands is already a party and which include investor-state dispute resolution, but also, for example, the European Court of Human Rights. In addition, everyone retains the option to opt for the national court instead of the ICS. Unlike the national court, the ICS cannot decide on the legality of a measure, but only to award compensation. Mr Van Dijk and Mr Beucker asked how I judged the claim of several lawyers who said that approval should take place with a two-thirds majority. As explained above in response to questions from your Chamber and the House of Representatives, the Government considers that there is no derogation from the Constitution under this Treaty and therefore no two-thirds majority is required for approval. The House of Representatives voted against a similar amendment. Mr Otten's question on the adoption of this proposal by a two-thirds majority in the Chamber. As I have just said, this Treaty does not derogate from the Constitution and therefore does not require a two-thirds majority. Then Mr Van Dijk asked: does the Minister recognise that there is and continues to be a process and decision that escapes the democratic process and the national legal order? No. In the government's view, there are no processes and decisions that evade a democratic process and the national legal order. In previous replies, the reasons why the ICS and the committees do not affect the democratic process and the national legal order, as I have just said, have already been addressed, because the Treaty provisions of CETA can only be amended by the contracting parties themselves. Those Treaty changes should be subject to approval in accordance with national constitutional procedures. Then the motion under letter M. I'd advise against it. What I have just said is that the government does not believe that there is any departure from the Constitution in this Treaty. In addition, Article 6, second paragraph of the Reichswet, provides that by way of derogation from the Constitution, the draft law for the adoption of a Treaty must reflect the approval being granted in compliance with the provisions of Article 91, third paragraph of the Constitution. If your Chamber, unlike the government and the House of Representatives, now believes that the Treaty is different from the Constitution, then it cannot do anything but reject the bill. The draft law does not contain the provision required by law in the event of a derogation from the Constitution. I also have a few other points, but there may still be questions about this.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u603	Yes. Mrs Prast asked about the difference between the ratification and implementation of CETA and the trade agreement between the EU and New Zealand. Is there any type of investor who would like to join the ICS alone? What are the characteristics of this investor? On 30 June, the EU and New Zealand announced a trade agreement. This is an agreement on trade agreements. Under the EU-New Zealand Trade Agreement, investors are entitled to equal treatment and market access, but there is no investment protection. Therefore, there is no access to dispute settlement between an investment and a State, as provided by the ICS under CETA. In this respect, therefore, this agreement is also different from CETA. The negotiated agreement has yet to be legally cleaned up and translated before being submitted to the Council for approval. It is not expected to happen until the end of this year. On the basis of the content of the agreement, the Council and the European Parliament will assess the agreement, but we cannot therefore distinguish between investors under ICS with CETA and investors under the terms of the trade agreement with New Zealand. Then Mrs Prast had another question about jobs: how to prevent more jobs from competing with care in other sectors? CETA has been provisionally applied for more than five years. Although bilateral trade with Canada is growing, changes in employment in one sector are not directly at the expense of those in another sector. Shifts taking place will take place in particular within sectors such as trade or transport and storage. Finally, CETA contains agreements on the temporary movement of persons for services, but these agreements are limited to certain professions for which a university degree or knowledge of equivalent level is required. Then Mr Van Apeldoorn asked about deregulation and the standstill clause. This is about market access for services. The aim of the standstill mechanism is to ensure that a market remains at least as open as it was at the time of the conclusion of the agreement. This contributes to predictability. In maintaining the liberalisation of services, it is important that Canadian companies in these sectors should be able to offer services in the same way as Dutch, German, French and other European companies. With the ratchet mechanism, certain future liberalising measures of a Party are automatically part of the Convention. This will not make them more stringent later on. The aim of this is that the chord remains forward looking, so in time. Liberalisation of a sector does not mean deregulating it. Parties may continue to regulate and impose requirements as long as this is not done through arbitrary or unfair discrimination. Then Mr Otten asked about the commitments made to the PvdA in exchange for support for CETA. That suggestion is far from my point of view. At the request of your Chamber, including the PvdA, I brought proposals for the introduction and improvement of the complaint mechanism to the attention of the Commission. This correspondence has been shared with your Chamber. Your Chamber was thus informed of the requests to the Commission. It is then up to the PvdA Group to consider and express an opinion on CETA. ParlaMint-NL_2022-07-11-eerstekamer-3.u742	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I would like to answer them as follows. First of all, I would like to address the questions on the Constitution. Then I come to the other questions that have been asked. Finally, I would like to end with the motion. Several members recalled whether the ICS would be in breach of the Constitution. I would like to be clear about CETA, the ICS and the Constitution. As has been said, the Cabinet considers that there is no conflict with the Constitution. The Council of State also makes this very clear. Mrs Karimi just asked me a question about national competence and investment protection in the first period. As I understand it, this was about the ruling of the EU Court of Justice in the Singapore case. In it, it ruled on the division of competences between the EU and the Member States, and considered that investment protection falls within the shared competence of the Union and the Member States. This has nothing to do with the Dutch Constitution, but with the interpretation of EU law. The fact that we as a Member State may agree to CETA and the establishment of ICS does not mean that we transfer a competence from the Netherlands to the international legal system. The EU Court of Justice also made it clear that ICS is not contrary to EU law. This was indicated by the Court of Justice in Wallonia's request for an opinion. Let me say again about the possible conflict with Article 112 of the Constitution. This has also been brought about by a number of parties and has led to further discussions. There is no question of that either, as has been said. That article deals with the division of competence within the national legal order. The ICS does not remove the dispute settlement of claims in the national legal order from national courts, as laid down in Article 112 of the Constitution. International agreements are being made on minimum legal protection for foreign investors. International judges in the ICS can rule on this. This is not a parallel process of justice, but a very specific additional or alternative option for investors to enforce their rights in specific cases on the basis of international agreements. I can only endorse what the Council of State has said. Then, in response to Mr Van Rooijen's questions, there is a risk of parallel justice and contradictory statements. He asked if this is desirable. I can reassure Mr Van Rooijen. Under CETA, there is no possibility of different rulings. The ICS Court shall have jurisdiction on the basis of CETA and national courts under national law. That is another legal basis. Parallel procedures are also not allowed under CETA. An investor cannot bring a claim before the ICS and at the same time conduct an ongoing procedure before the national courts. In addition, the judges under ICS may not rule on the legality of a particular measure and may only consider the interpretation of national law as a factual fact. They can only compensate for a breach of the CETA rules, i.e. if a government fails to comply with the principles of good governance. Mr Koole talked about the costs of ICS and SMEs. ICS is much cheaper for parties than ISDS. It is generally more expensive than national law, but the agreement in CETA is to establish special rules on costs for SMEs. The European Commission is working this out now and must be ready for the entry into force of the ICS; we have already established that ICS has not yet entered into force. All this is intended to facilitate access for SMEs to the ICS. Mr Van Apeldoorn asked me how many SMEs use CETA, relative to the total number of SMEs, and what the trading volume of SMEs is. Around 3,700 SMEs currently use CETA. This would represent around 7% of the total number of SMEs with more than 10 employees in the Netherlands. In practice, SMEs are often active in international markets. If SMEs using CETA are compared to the number of medium-sized SMEs, this share is much higher. As far as the volume of trade is concerned, Eurostat figures show that in 2019 more than 70% of exports to Canada were made by Dutch SMEs. Then Mrs. Prast. I'd come back to the health jobs. People cannot work in care if there are more jobs in trade. Growth in bilateral trade between the EU and Canada does not mean that this is entirely new jobs, but part of employment will also be related to trade diversion, as I said earlier. The impact of CETA on the overall labour market is also relatively limited compared to the impact of overall trade and the impact of macroeconomic factors. It is therefore not self-evident that all people who work in the trade can, or will, work in the care sector. There is also no question of full labour mobility. Over time, there is no fixed number of available labour in the Netherlands. Mrs Faber and Mrs Karimi asked: farmers get access to a new power, but what good is it if you are limited by nitrogen; how does the Minister see this? The transition of the agricultural sector and the area-based approach will undoubtedly also have an impact on our international market. This means that volumes will change in imports and exports, but the international market will continue to play an important role in earning power in part of the sector. The cabinet focuses on the export of high-quality products. Canada is a developed economy with a demand for high-quality products. So here are opportunities for Dutch business. More opportunities for trade are therefore becoming more important. I shall then go straight to Mrs Huizinga's question. Farmers are of course concerned about their future, but farmers have also expressed concern about CETA. Are they now disadvantaged by nitrogen measures and what measures can be taken by the EU? Our farmers must meet high sustainability requirements. That is why, like the Minister of LNV, I think this is an important issue, because our farmers are very innovative. The EU has a system of rules that protects but also makes the internal market resilient, including protection for sensitive sectors and/or products. The EU and the Netherlands continuously monitor markets for distortions. In the event of a threatening sudden increase in imports, the EU, as I have just said, can take trade defensive measures such as safeguard measures. The transition of the agricultural sector and the area-based approach will undoubtedly also have an impact on our international market. This may mean that volumes will change in imports and exports, but the international market will continue to play an important role in earning power in part of the sector. The cabinet focuses on the export of high-quality products. Canada is a developed economy with a demand for high-quality products. So here are opportunities for Dutch business. Canada also has ambitious nitrogen targets, including for agriculture. In order to ensure that Canada achieves this objective, it published the Emissions Reduction Plan for 2030 in March 2022. Mrs. Prast asked about New Zealand and the ICS. If it is so important with Canada, why is it not so important with New Zealand? Indeed, investment protection is not part of the EU-New Zealand Treaty, nor has it been part of the negotiations. Unlike CETA, where Canada and the EU had a desire to include investment protection in the Convention, New Zealand, unlike Canada, has also not concluded individual investment agreements with EU Member States and therefore no interest in agreeing on these agreements with the EU. There is no need to replace anything there. Mrs. Prast asked for five more years of CETA without ICS. She said: there are apparently investors who only invest with ICS; what type of investors are they? The fact that five years of CETA have passed without ICS cannot automatically lead to the conclusion that there are therefore investors who only invest with ICS. It cannot be said whether and which type of investors would only invest in the EU because of ICS from Canada or vice versa. In addition, ICS exists in case of a risk of failure with an investment, and there would be a breach of the principles of good governance as laid down under CETA. Mr Otten asked for Rule 26. He said that the Committee has the power to decide on all parts of the Convention; decisions of the Joint Committee are binding on all parties and the Parliament would not have any influence on that. Article 26.3 provides that in order to achieve the objectives of CETA, the Joint Committee is to be empowered to take decisions on all matters identified in this Agreement, i.e. only those explicitly mentioned in the Convention. Subsequently, the text of the treaty always indicates where this can be done in different places. CETA only provides for the competence of the Joint Committee to amend certain technical protocols and annexes. This concerns, for example, the addition, as I said earlier, of new geographical indications on the list in Annex 20a. For the sake of clarity, most of the issues that may be decided by the Joint Committee concern exclusive EU powers. These decisions of the Joint Committee therefore require the Union's agreement, i.e. through the Council, and that includes the Netherlands. About what the Netherlands does there, the cabinet is accountable to parliament. For the rest, CETA Treaty provisions can only be amended by the Contracting Parties themselves and treaty changes must be approved in accordance with national constitutional procedures. Mr Van Dijk asked earlier today whether CETA is necessary to ensure compliance with the Paris Agreement by Canada. No, according to the evaluation of the SGP, does the Minister agree? Under CETA, Contracting Parties confirm that they will fulfil their obligations under multilateral environmental agreements and commit themselves to the effective implementation of, inter alia, the Paris Agreement. In a joint recommendation, they reaffirmed the importance of the Paris Agreement with a view to cooperation in the field of climate and trade. In doing so, CETA provides an additional leverage for compliance with international environmental agreements such as Paris. I am confident that Canada will fulfil its obligations under Paris. Mr Schalk asked whether the ICS was a system in accordance with the Constitution. The answer is yes. Mr Van Rooijen asked which third countries the five non-EU and non-Canada judges came from. The Judges shall be appointed by the Joint Committee. As far as non-EU/Canada countries are concerned, there is no definition of the five countries from which these judges will come. The Joint Committee shall be composed of representatives of the EU and Canada. The list of candidates for judges is thus drawn up jointly by the EU and Canada. It is stipulated that the courts must comply with strict requirements, including requirements of independence, a code of conduct, and the requirements of their country to hold or be known as an expert lawyer. Mr Schalk also asked about the competitiveness of the Dutch farmer and about the strict rules on Canadian farmers. As I mentioned earlier, agricultural entrepreneurs have to comply with different standards that differ from one country to another. Eventually, there are a multitude of factors involved in determining the final competitive position of Canadian and Dutch companies. Mr Beuker asked why we are dealing with CETA here if there is no transfer of competence. He also asked: why not the Vietnam agreement? CETA is dealt with in the First Chamber because it is a mixed agreement, where there are EU competences and Member State competences. That is why the national parliaments must also agree. This has nothing to do with the transfer of powers through CETA; that is not the case. The trade agreement with Vietnam or with Japan contained only agreements which fall within the exclusive competence of the Union. They will therefore only be submitted to the European Parliament. Again, there is no question of a transfer of powers through CETA. Mr Van Apeldoorn asked whether the harmonisation of product standards could be achieved through CETA. The EU and Canada may discuss possible regulatory cooperation under CETA. This means, therefore, to explore voluntarily the issues on which cooperation can be developed in the future. But the bottomline remains: changes on the EU side or new requirements always require EU legislative procedures. Mrs Prast asked another question: that was an open question from the first term or a Canadian bank that wants to take over a Dutch bank and does not get a licence, can go to the ICS because of lost profits. No, a Canadian bank that wants to take over a Dutch bank and doesn't get a license for that can't go to the ICS. When making an investment, each investor must comply with national legislation of the country in which the investment is made. Having a permit is a condition for making that investment. The refusal of that authorisation is not a ground for making a claim. Ms Prast also asked if the AFM and DNB had been consulted on CETA. She also asked about their views. The cabinet position for the signing and approval of CETA is broadly aligned within the government, including with the Ministry of Finance. That is, as Mrs Prast also knows, the mother ministry of DNB and AFM. During the negotiation process, there has also been constant alignment. None of these consultations raised any objections to CETA by or on behalf of the AFM and DNB. Mrs Prast also asked about the sustainability agreements and whether the Cabinet had properly informed the Chamber of the enforceability of these agreements in CETA. I have just emphasised that the sustainability agreements are legally binding. I have also indicated how they can be enforced under CETA. For the sake of clarity, I will explain once again the specific dispute resolution process provided for by CETA. In the case of alleged violations, one party may request formal consultations with the other party. If the consultations do not lead to a solution, an independent expert panel may be invoked. This panel of experts draws up a report assessing the alleged infringement and makes recommendations for compliance with the agreements. If the Panel of Experts considers that one of the Parties does not comply with its obligations, the Parties shall consult under the Convention and shall be deemed to identify appropriate measures or adopt an action plan within three months. There is therefore an obligation to follow up such a panel report. The Committee on Trade and Sustainable Development then monitors its follow-up. The expert panel report shall also be published. Under international law, the parties must comply with treaty obligations in good faith. The appreciation of the cabinet is that this process allows parties to a sufficient extent to enforce compliance. Then Mr Beuker asked whether we have a right of veto in the Council or whether it is decided by a simple majority. That depends on the subject. In some cases unanimity is required and in some cases it is not. This depends on the subject under EU law. Mrs Karimi then asked about the role of the Joint Committee in the field of standardisation.

Then, Mrs Karimi, on the role of the Joint Committee in the harmonisation of standards. A decision of the Joint Committee shall be taken by common accord. As a member of the Council, the Netherlands also has a say in the decision-making process. If I interpret it in such a way that I share the decision with your Chamber, so that you can decide on it before the Netherlands votes in the Council, then the answer to the motion is yes. So far.
